[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.8, F32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because my mind keeps going blank, and it’s been scaring me. It’s not just forgetting a word or walking into a room and forgetting why—I mean, I’ll be at work, looking at the road-closure forms or holding the stop-slow paddle, things I’ve done for years, and suddenly I just… can’t remember the steps. Like my head goes empty right when I need it not to.\n\nIt happens at the worst times, when I’m supposed to know exactly what to do, and then I start trying to force myself to remember, but that almost makes it worse. I’ll stare at the paperwork or the sign, and it feels like everything I know is just out of reach. That’s really why I’m here. I don’t feel like I can keep brushing it off.\n\nDoctor: Do these blank moments seem to happen more often when you're under a lot of stress or pressure?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think pressure makes it worse, or at least that’s when I notice it the most, because there’s no room to cover for it. Like if I’m just at home and my mind goes blank, I can pause or retrace what I was doing, but at work it’s different. I’ll be looking right at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and it’s something I’ve done for years, like I should know it automatically, and suddenly I just can’t remember the steps.\n\nAnd then because I know I’m supposed to act quickly and get it right, I start panicking inside and trying to force my brain to come back online, but it’s like the harder I try, the emptier it gets. It’s not just ordinary distraction. It feels like the information is there somewhere, but I can’t reach it when I need it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed outside of these blank moments, in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that. I mean, I know I get wound up when those blank moments happen, because it feels like I’m going to mess something up or look incompetent, especially at work. But outside of that, in just normal daily life, I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly.\n\nI do feel overwhelmed sometimes, but I haven’t really separated it out in my head, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be going through the day and I know I’m tense, or I feel like I have too much to keep track of, but I don’t always notice it until something goes wrong or I freeze up. Then I look back and think, “Maybe I was already overloaded,” but in the moment it just feels like I’m trying to get through things.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. I don’t have a clear answer. I know the blanking out scares me, and the pressure around it makes me feel overwhelmed. Outside of that, I haven’t really paid enough attention to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience memory problems or difficulty recalling information in situations outside of your work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure if it happens as much outside work, or maybe I just don’t notice it the same way because there isn’t someone waiting on me or traffic depending on me. At work it’s very obvious because I’ll be looking right at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. It’s like my mind just goes completely blank at the worst possible time.\n\nOutside work… I mean, I do have moments where I feel like I lose the thread of what I’m doing, but the ones that really scare me are the work ones because they’re so basic to my job. It’s not like I’m learning something new. I know those procedures. I’ve done them over and over, and then suddenly it’s like I can’t access them. That’s the part that made me come in, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body watching yourself during these blank moments?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t think I’d describe it like being outside my body exactly. Not like I’m up on the ceiling watching myself or anything like that. It’s more that everything sort of stops in my head, and I’m still there, I know I’m there, I know I’m holding the paddle or looking at the paperwork, but I can’t connect to what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nSometimes afterward I think back on it and it feels kind of unreal, but in the moment I’m mostly just frozen and embarrassed and trying to make myself function. I’m aware of my body, like I can feel myself standing there, and I can hear things going on around me, but it’s like there’s a delay between me and my own thinking.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed the “watching myself from outside” feeling. If anything, it’s more like I’m trapped inside my head while it’s not working properly, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in a daze or disconnected from your surroundings when these blank moments happen?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes, but I don’t know if “disconnected from my surroundings” is exactly the word I’d use. It’s more like my mind just shuts off right in the middle of something I should know how to do. Like I’ll be standing there with the stop-slow paddle, or looking at the road-closure forms, and suddenly I can’t remember the next step. And these are things I’ve done for years, so it’s not like I’m confused because it’s new.\n\nI’m still aware of where I am, like I know I’m at work and I know cars are there and people are expecting me to do what I’m supposed to do. But it feels like I’m in this mental fog, or like there’s nothing available in my head for a few moments. That’s what scares me. It happens at the worst times, when I need my brain to work, and instead it just goes blank.\n\nSo maybe “dazed” fits more than disconnected. I don’t feel totally gone, but I feel stuck, like I’m there but I can’t get my thoughts to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like things around you aren't real during these blank moments?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say things around me aren’t real. I don’t think I’ve had that exact feeling, like the cars or the cones or the people around me are fake or like I’m in a dream or something. It’s more that everything is happening and I know it’s happening, but I can’t quite process it fast enough in that moment.\n\nLike, I’ll still recognize where I am. I know I’m at the job site, I know the road is there, I know there are drivers waiting, and I know I’m supposed to be doing something. It doesn’t look unreal to me, exactly. It’s just like my brain stops connecting the pieces together, and then because I’m stuck like that, the whole situation can feel strange afterward when I think about it.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’d describe it as the world not feeling real. More like I feel foggy and frozen while everything around me keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Have these blank moments affected your relationships with coworkers or your ability to perform your job overall?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s affected it a lot. More than I wanted to admit for a long time.\n\nAt work, I used to be someone people could count on. I mean, I know the job, I’ve done the traffic-safety steps so many times, and I understand how serious it is because if you hesitate or miss something, it’s not just embarrassing, it can be dangerous. But with these blank moments, I stopped trusting myself. I’d get stuck on things like the road-closure paperwork, where I couldn’t finish it or I’d have to stare at it and keep checking because I couldn’t hold the steps in my head. And out on the road, I’d worry, “What if I freeze right when I need to signal or move people through safely?” That kind of fear made me less reliable, and I think other people noticed.\n\nWith coworkers, I started pulling back. I didn’t want them to see me mess up or have to cover for me, and I was ashamed, so I’d get quiet or avoid talking more than I had to. I could feel myself becoming someone they couldn’t depend on in the same way, and that hurt because I wasn’t trying to be careless. I just couldn’t make my brain cooperate.\n\nAnd it didn’t stay only at work. I pulled away from choir and volunteering too, and even from my family. I kept telling myself I just needed to get it together, but I wasn’t really functioning like myself. Eventually my family got worried enough that they felt like they had to bring me in for help, because I wasn’t handling things, not at work and not outside of it either.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or how you're functioning in your daily life outside of work since these blank moments started happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… outside of work it changed a lot too. I think at first I tried to tell myself it was just stress from the job, or being tired, or that I could sort of contain it there, but it didn’t stay in that box.\n\nI stopped doing things I used to show up for. Choir was one of the first things I pulled back from, because I didn’t trust myself to keep up or even just be present the way I normally would. Volunteering too — I used to care about being dependable, being someone who came when I said I would, but I started canceling or avoiding it because I felt like I couldn’t count on my own mind. It’s embarrassing to say that, because those were things that made me feel useful.\n\nAnd with my family, I withdrew. I wasn’t talking as much, wasn’t participating like I used to. I think I kept trying to hide how badly I was functioning, but that probably made it more obvious. They could tell I wasn’t myself. I’d get stuck, or overwhelmed, or just not follow through on normal things, and eventually they got worried enough that they felt like they had to bring me in for help.\n\nSo yes, it wasn’t just the road-closure paperwork or not trusting myself with the traffic-safety steps at work. It spread into the rest of my life, where I stopped being reliable and started pulling away from almost everyone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of sadness or hopelessness lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. It’s been pretty constant lately, especially in the mornings.\n\nMost mornings I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow sadness sitting in my chest before I’ve even really done anything. It’s like the day already feels ruined before I put my feet on the floor. I’ll open my eyes and for a second I’m just lying there, and instead of feeling like, okay, I need to get up and start the day, it’s more like this sinking feeling, like I already don’t have enough in me for whatever the day is going to ask from me.\n\nAnd it’s not just regular being tired or having a bad mood, at least it doesn’t feel that way. It feels deeper than that, like something is weighing me down from the inside. I don’t always have a clear reason for it right when I wake up, which almost makes it worse, because then I start thinking, “Why do I feel this awful already?” But it’s there before anything even happens.\n\nHopelessness… I guess that’s part of it too. Not every second, but when I wake up feeling like that, it’s hard not to feel like nothing is going to get better that day. It’s like I’m starting from behind before the day even begins.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about a year and two months, I think. Around that long where waking up already feeling heavy and empty became more normal than not.\n\nIt wasn’t like one single morning where I thought, “This is the start of something.” It kind of settled in and then stayed. But looking back, the heavy depression, the guilt, the low energy, and not really having much appetite… that whole pattern has been with me for about a year and two months now. Some days are a little different in intensity, but that morning sadness has been there for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Does the heavy, hollow sadness you feel in the mornings tend to stay with you throughout the day or does it change?\n\nPatient: It usually starts right when I wake up, before anything has even happened, and that’s the part that makes it feel so… unfair, I guess. Like I’ll open my eyes and there’s already this heavy, hollow sadness in my chest, and my first thought isn’t even a thought exactly — it’s more like this feeling that the day is already ruined before I’ve even put my feet on the floor.\n\nAs the day goes on, it can shift a little. Sometimes I can get distracted for a while or go through the motions, but it doesn’t feel like it completely leaves. It’s more like it’s sitting underneath everything. The morning is usually when it feels the most obvious and crushing, because there’s nothing to blame it on yet. I haven’t done anything wrong, nothing has happened, but I already feel like I’m starting the day buried under it.\n\nSo… it changes in intensity, I guess, but that heaviness tends to follow me. It’s not like I wake up sad and then by lunch I’m suddenly fine. It’s there in the background, and sometimes it comes back up really strong again if I stop and notice it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I have. A lot, actually.

I used to be the person who was always in the middle of things, not in a loud way maybe, but dependable. Like with choir, I’d be the one figuring out rides, texting people, making sure somebody’s kid wasn’t left without a way there. Or with the garden swaps, I’d be asking who had extra seedlings, who needed jars, who was bringing what. And youth basketball snacks too — I used to keep track of whose turn it was, remind the other parents, sometimes just do it myself if somebody forgot.

And now it’s like… I see the messages come in, and I just don’t feel that push in me to answer. I can sit there for hours with my phone right next to me, or even in my hand, staring at the screen, and I know I should reply. I know people are probably waiting on me. But there’s this blankness, like the part of me that used to care enough to jump up and organize things is just not connecting.

It’s not that I suddenly hate those things or those people. That’s what makes me feel bad about it. I remember enjoying being useful and involved. I remember feeling like, okay, this is where I fit. But lately it feels far away, like something I used to do because I was a different version of myself. Now I just sit there and let the phone light up, and I don’t answer.

I know the blanking out scares me, and the pressure around it makes me feel overwhelmed. Outside of that, I haven’t really paid enough attention to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience memory problems or difficulty recalling information in situations outside of your work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure if it happens as much outside work, or maybe I just don’t notice it the same way because there isn’t someone waiting on me or traffic depending on me. At work it’s very obvious because I’ll be looking right at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. That’s the part that made me come in, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body watching yourself during these blank moments?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t think I’d describe it like being outside my body exactly. Not like I’m up on the ceiling watching myself or anything like that. It’s more that everything sort of stops in my head, and I’m still there, I know I’m there, I know I’m holding the paddle or looking at the paperwork, but I can’t connect to what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nSometimes afterward I think back on it and it feels kind of unreal, but in the moment I’m mostly just frozen and embarrassed and trying to make myself function. I’m aware of my body, like I can feel myself standing there, and I can hear things going on around me, but it’s like there’s a delay between me and my own thinking.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed the “watching myself from outside” feeling. If anything, it’s more like I’m trapped inside my head while it’s not working properly, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in a daze or disconnected from your surroundings when these blank moments happen?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes, but I don’t know if “disconnected from my surroundings” is exactly the word I’d use. Like I’ll be standing there with the stop-slow paddle, or looking at the road-closure forms, and suddenly I can’t remember the next step. But it feels like I’m in this mental fog, or like there’s nothing available in my head for a few moments. That’s what scares me. It happens at the worst times, when I need my brain to work, and instead it just goes blank.\n\nSo maybe “dazed” fits more than disconnected. I don’t feel totally gone, but I feel stuck, like I’m there but I can’t get my thoughts to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like things around you aren't real during these blank moments?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say things around me aren’t real. I don’t think I’ve had that exact feeling, like the cars or the cones or the people around me are fake or like I’m in a dream or something. I know I’m at the job site, I know the road is there, I know there are drivers waiting, and I know I’m supposed to be doing something. It doesn’t look unreal to me, exactly. It’s just like my brain stops connecting the pieces together, and then because I’m stuck like that, the whole situation can feel strange afterward when I think about it.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’d describe it as the world not feeling real. More like I feel foggy and frozen while everything around me keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Have these blank moments affected your relationships with coworkers or your ability to perform your job overall?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s affected it a lot. I mean, I know the job, I’ve done the traffic-safety steps so many times, and I understand how serious it is because if you hesitate or miss something, it’s not just embarrassing, it can be dangerous. But with these blank moments, I stopped trusting myself. I’d get stuck on things like the road-closure paperwork, where I couldn’t finish it or I’d have to stare at it and keep checking because I couldn’t hold the steps in my head. And out on the road, I’d worry, “What if I freeze right when I need to signal or move people through safely?” That kind of fear made me less reliable, and I think other people noticed.\n\nWith coworkers, I started pulling back. I didn’t want them to see me mess up or have to cover for me, and I was ashamed, so I’d get quiet or avoid talking more than I had to. I could feel myself becoming someone they couldn’t depend on in the same way, and that hurt because I wasn’t trying to be careless. I just couldn’t make my brain cooperate.\n\nAnd it didn’t stay only at work. I pulled away from choir and volunteering too, and even from my family. I kept telling myself I just needed to get it together, but I wasn’t really functioning like myself. Eventually my family got worried enough that they felt like they had to bring me in for help, because I wasn’t handling things, not at work and not outside of it either.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or how you're functioning in your daily life outside of work since these blank moments started happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… outside of work it changed a lot too. I think at first I tried to tell myself it was just stress from the job, or being tired, or that I could sort of contain it there, but it didn’t stay in that box.\n\nI stopped doing things I used to show up for. Choir was one of the first things I pulled back from, because I didn’t trust myself to keep up or even just be present the way I normally would. Volunteering too — I used to care about being dependable, being someone who came when I said I would, but I started canceling or avoiding it because I felt like I couldn’t count on my own mind. It’s embarrassing to say that, because those were things that made me feel useful.\n\nAnd with my family, I withdrew. I wasn’t talking as much, wasn’t participating like I used to. I think I kept trying to hide how badly I was functioning, but that probably made it more obvious. They could tell I wasn’t myself. I’d get stuck, or overwhelmed, or just not follow through on normal things, and eventually they got worried enough that they felt like they had to bring me in for help.\n\nSo yes, it wasn’t just the road-closure paperwork or not trusting myself with the traffic-safety steps at work. Not every second, but when I wake up feeling like that, it’s hard not to feel like nothing is going to get better that day. Around that long where waking up already feeling heavy and empty became more normal than not.\n\nIt wasn’t like one single morning where I thought, “This is the start of something.” It kind of settled in and then stayed. But looking back, the heavy depression, the guilt, the low energy, and not really having much appetite… that whole pattern has been with me for about a year and two months now. Some days are a little different in intensity, but that morning sadness has been there for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Does the heavy, hollow sadness you feel in the mornings tend to stay with you throughout the day or does it change?\n\nPatient: It usually starts right when I wake up, before anything has even happened, and that’s the part that makes it feel so… unfair, I guess. Like I’ll open my eyes and there’s already this heavy, hollow sadness in my chest, and my first thought isn’t even a thought exactly — it’s more like this feeling that the day is already ruined before I’ve even put my feet on the floor.\n\nAs the day goes on, it can shift a little. Sometimes I can get distracted for a while or go through the motions, but it doesn’t feel like it completely leaves. It’s more like it’s sitting underneath everything. The morning is usually when it feels the most obvious and crushing, because there’s nothing to blame it on yet. I haven’t done anything wrong, nothing has happened, but I already feel like I’m starting the day buried under it.\n\nSo… it changes in intensity, I guess, but that heaviness tends to follow me. It’s not like I wake up sad and then by lunch I’m suddenly fine. It’s there in the background, and sometimes it comes back up really strong again if I stop and notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy since these blank moments and feelings of sadness started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. A lot, actually.\n\nI used to be the person who was always in the middle of things, not in a loud way maybe, but dependable. Like with choir, I’d be the one figuring out rides, texting people, making sure somebody’s kid wasn’t left without a way there. Or with the garden swaps, I’d be asking who had extra seedlings, who needed jars, who was bringing what. And youth basketball snacks too — I used to keep track of whose turn it was, remind the other parents, sometimes just do it myself if somebody forgot.\n\nAnd now it’s like… I see the messages come in, and I just don’t feel that push in me to answer. I can sit there for hours with my phone right next to me, or even in my hand, staring at the screen, and I know I should reply. I know people are probably waiting on me. But there’s this blankness, like the part of me that used to care enough to jump up and organize things is just not connecting.\n\nIt’s not that I suddenly hate those things or those people. That’s what makes me feel bad about it. I remember enjoying being useful and involved. I remember feeling like, okay, this is where I fit. But lately it feels far away, like something I used to do because I was a different version of myself. Now I just sit there and let the phone light up, and I don’t answer.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But looking back, the heavy depression, the guilt, the low energy, and not really having much appetite… that whole pattern has been with me for about a year and two months now. Some days are a little different in intensity, but that morning sadness has been there for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Does the heavy, hollow sadness you feel in the mornings tend to stay with you throughout the day or does it change?\n\nPatient: It usually starts right when I wake up, before anything has even happened, and that’s the part that makes it feel so… unfair, I guess. Like I’ll open my eyes and there’s already this heavy, hollow sadness in my chest, and my first thought isn’t even a thought exactly — it’s more like this feeling that the day is already ruined before I’ve even put my feet on the floor.\n\nAs the day goes on, it can shift a little. Sometimes I can get distracted for a while or go through the motions, but it doesn’t feel like it completely leaves. It’s more like it’s sitting underneath everything. The morning is usually when it feels the most obvious and crushing, because there’s nothing to blame it on yet. I haven’t done anything wrong, nothing has happened, but I already feel like I’m starting the day buried under it.\n\nSo… it changes in intensity, I guess, but that heaviness tends to follow me. It’s not like I wake up sad and then by lunch I’m suddenly fine. It’s there in the background, and sometimes it comes back up really strong again if I stop and notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy since these blank moments and feelings of sadness started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. A lot, actually.\n\nI used to be the person who was always in the middle of things, not in a loud way maybe, but dependable. Like with choir, I’d be the one figuring out rides, texting people, making sure somebody’s kid wasn’t left without a way there. Or with the garden swaps, I’d be asking who had extra seedlings, who needed jars, who was bringing what. And youth basketball snacks too — I used to keep track of whose turn it was, remind the other parents, sometimes just do it myself if somebody forgot.\n\nAnd now it’s like… I see the messages come in, and I just don’t feel that push in me to answer. I can sit there for hours with my phone right next to me, or even in my hand, staring at the screen, and I know I should reply. I know people are probably waiting on me. But there’s this blankness, like the part of me that used to care enough to jump up and organize things is just not connecting.\n\nIt’s not that I suddenly hate those things or those people. That’s what makes me feel bad about it. I remember enjoying being useful and involved. I remember feeling like, okay, this is where I fit. But lately it feels far away, like something I used to do because I was a different version of myself. Now I just sit there and let the phone light up, and I don’t answer.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any significant changes in your weight lately?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 41 / crossing_guard_or_flagger / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Weight_Appetite_Change\nmanifestation: \"Food has stopped meaning much to me, and I skip meals without noticing until my flagger pants are loose and my family starts asking why I look so drawn.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself regularly, so I can’t give you an exact number, but I’ve noticed it in my clothes. My flagger pants — the ones I wear for work — they’ve been sitting looser on me, like I keep having to pull them up or adjust them more than I used to. At first I thought maybe they’d stretched out or something, but then other clothes started feeling kind of the same.

Food just… doesn’t mean much to me lately. I don’t really think about it the way I used to. I’ll realize it’s late in the day and I haven’t eaten, not because I was trying not to, just because it didn’t occur to me or nothing sounded worth the effort. Sometimes my family will ask if I ate, or they’ll say I look drawn, like my face looks thinner or tired or something, and that makes me feel embarrassed because I didn’t really notice it happening.

So yes, I think I’ve lost weight. I just don’t know how much.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F43.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because my mind keeps going blank, and it’s been scaring me. It’s not just forgetting a word or walking into a room and forgetting why—I mean, I’ll be at work, looking at the road-closure forms or holding the stop-slow paddle, things I’ve done for years, and suddenly I just… can’t remember the steps. Like my head goes empty right when I need it not to.\n\nIt happens at the worst times, when I’m supposed to know exactly what to do, and then I start trying to force myself to remember, but that almost makes it worse. I’ll stare at the paperwork or the sign, and it feels like everything I know is just out of reach. That’s really why I’m here. I don’t feel like I can keep brushing it off.\n\nDoctor: Do these blank moments seem to happen more often when you're under a lot of stress or pressure?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think pressure makes it worse, or at least that’s when I notice it the most, because there’s no room to cover for it. Like if I’m just at home and my mind goes blank, I can pause or retrace what I was doing, but at work it’s different. I’ll be looking right at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and it’s something I’ve done for years, like I should know it automatically, and suddenly I just can’t remember the steps.\n\nAnd then because I know I’m supposed to act quickly and get it right, I start panicking inside and trying to force my brain to come back online, but it’s like the harder I try, the emptier it gets. It’s not just ordinary distraction. It feels like the information is there somewhere, but I can’t reach it when I need it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed outside of these blank moments, in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that. I mean, I know I get wound up when those blank moments happen, because it feels like I’m going to mess something up or look incompetent, especially at work. But outside of that, in just normal daily life, I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly.\n\nI do feel overwhelmed sometimes, but I haven’t really separated it out in my head, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be going through the day and I know I’m tense, or I feel like I have too much to keep track of, but I don’t always notice it until something goes wrong or I freeze up. Then I look back and think, “Maybe I was already overloaded,” but in the moment it just feels like I’m trying to get through things.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. I don’t have a clear answer. I know the blanking out scares me, and the pressure around it makes me feel overwhelmed. Outside of that, I haven’t really paid enough attention to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience memory problems or difficulty recalling information in situations outside of your work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure if it happens as much outside work, or maybe I just don’t notice it the same way because there isn’t someone waiting on me or traffic depending on me. At work it’s very obvious because I’ll be looking right at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. It’s like my mind just goes completely blank at the worst possible time.\n\nOutside work… I mean, I do have moments where I feel like I lose the thread of what I’m doing, but the ones that really scare me are the work ones because they’re so basic to my job. It’s not like I’m learning something new. I know those procedures. I’ve done them over and over, and then suddenly it’s like I can’t access them. That’s the part that made me come in, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body watching yourself during these blank moments?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t think I’d describe it like being outside my body exactly. Not like I’m up on the ceiling watching myself or anything like that. It’s more that everything sort of stops in my head, and I’m still there, I know I’m there, I know I’m holding the paddle or looking at the paperwork, but I can’t connect to what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nSometimes afterward I think back on it and it feels kind of unreal, but in the moment I’m mostly just frozen and embarrassed and trying to make myself function. I’m aware of my body, like I can feel myself standing there, and I can hear things going on around me, but it’s like there’s a delay between me and my own thinking.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed the “watching myself from outside” feeling. If anything, it’s more like I’m trapped inside my head while it’s not working properly, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in a daze or disconnected from your surroundings when these blank moments happen?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes, but I don’t know if “disconnected from my surroundings” is exactly the word I’d use. It’s more like my mind just shuts off right in the middle of something I should know how to do. Like I’ll be standing there with the stop-slow paddle, or looking at the road-closure forms, and suddenly I can’t remember the next step. And these are things I’ve done for years, so it’s not like I’m confused because it’s new.\n\nI’m still aware of where I am, like I know I’m at work and I know cars are there and people are expecting me to do what I’m supposed to do. But it feels like I’m in this mental fog, or like there’s nothing available in my head for a few moments. That’s what scares me. It happens at the worst times, when I need my brain to work, and instead it just goes blank.\n\nSo maybe “dazed” fits more than disconnected. I don’t feel totally gone, but I feel stuck, like I’m there but I can’t get my thoughts to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like things around you aren't real during these blank moments?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say things around me aren’t real. I don’t think I’ve had that exact feeling, like the cars or the cones or the people around me are fake or like I’m in a dream or something. It’s more that everything is happening and I know it’s happening, but I can’t quite process it fast enough in that moment.\n\nLike, I’ll still recognize where I am. I know I’m at the job site, I know the road is there, I know there are drivers waiting, and I know I’m supposed to be doing something. It doesn’t look unreal to me, exactly. It’s just like my brain stops connecting the pieces together, and then because I’m stuck like that, the whole situation can feel strange afterward when I think about it.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’d describe it as the world not feeling real. More like I feel foggy and frozen while everything around me keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Have these blank moments affected your relationships with coworkers or your ability to perform your job overall?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s affected it a lot. More than I wanted to admit for a long time.\n\nAt work, I used to be someone people could count on. I mean, I know the job, I’ve done the traffic-safety steps so many times, and I understand how serious it is because if you hesitate or miss something, it’s not just embarrassing, it can be dangerous. But with these blank moments, I stopped trusting myself. I’d get stuck on things like the road-closure paperwork, where I couldn’t finish it or I’d have to stare at it and keep checking because I couldn’t hold the steps in my head. And out on the road, I’d worry, “What if I freeze right when I need to signal or move people through safely?” That kind of fear made me less reliable, and I think other people noticed.\n\nWith coworkers, I started pulling back. I didn’t want them to see me mess up or have to cover for me, and I was ashamed, so I’d get quiet or avoid talking more than I had to. I could feel myself becoming someone they couldn’t depend on in the same way, and that hurt because I wasn’t trying to be careless. I just couldn’t make my brain cooperate.\n\nAnd it didn’t stay only at work. I pulled away from choir and volunteering too, and even from my family. I kept telling myself I just needed to get it together, but I wasn’t really functioning like myself. Eventually my family got worried enough that they felt like they had to bring me in for help, because I wasn’t handling things, not at work and not outside of it either.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or how you're functioning in your daily life outside of work since these blank moments started happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… outside of work it changed a lot too. I think at first I tried to tell myself it was just stress from the job, or being tired, or that I could sort of contain it there, but it didn’t stay in that box.\n\nI stopped doing things I used to show up for. Choir was one of the first things I pulled back from, because I didn’t trust myself to keep up or even just be present the way I normally would. Volunteering too — I used to care about being dependable, being someone who came when I said I would, but I started canceling or avoiding it because I felt like I couldn’t count on my own mind. It’s embarrassing to say that, because those were things that made me feel useful.\n\nAnd with my family, I withdrew. I wasn’t talking as much, wasn’t participating like I used to. I think I kept trying to hide how badly I was functioning, but that probably made it more obvious. They could tell I wasn’t myself. I’d get stuck, or overwhelmed, or just not follow through on normal things, and eventually they got worried enough that they felt like they had to bring me in for help.\n\nSo yes, it wasn’t just the road-closure paperwork or not trusting myself with the traffic-safety steps at work. It spread into the rest of my life, where I stopped being reliable and started pulling away from almost everyone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of sadness or hopelessness lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. It’s been pretty constant lately, especially in the mornings.\n\nMost mornings I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow sadness sitting in my chest before I’ve even really done anything. It’s like the day already feels ruined before I put my feet on the floor. I’ll open my eyes and for a second I’m just lying there, and instead of feeling like, okay, I need to get up and start the day, it’s more like this sinking feeling, like I already don’t have enough in me for whatever the day is going to ask from me.\n\nAnd it’s not just regular being tired or having a bad mood, at least it doesn’t feel that way. It feels deeper than that, like something is weighing me down from the inside. I don’t always have a clear reason for it right when I wake up, which almost makes it worse, because then I start thinking, “Why do I feel this awful already?” But it’s there before anything even happens.\n\nHopelessness… I guess that’s part of it too. Not every second, but when I wake up feeling like that, it’s hard not to feel like nothing is going to get better that day. It’s like I’m starting from behind before the day even begins.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about a year and two months, I think. Like I’ll open my eyes and there’s already this heavy, hollow sadness in my chest, and my first thought isn’t even a thought exactly — it’s more like this feeling that the day is already ruined before I’ve even put my feet on the floor.\n\nAs the day goes on, it can shift a little. Sometimes I can get distracted for a while or go through the motions, but it doesn’t feel like it completely leaves. It’s more like it’s sitting underneath everything. The morning is usually when it feels the most obvious and crushing, because there’s nothing to blame it on yet. I haven’t done anything wrong, nothing has happened, but I already feel like I’m starting the day buried under it.\n\nSo… it changes in intensity, I guess, but that heaviness tends to follow me. It’s not like I wake up sad and then by lunch I’m suddenly fine. It’s there in the background, and sometimes it comes back up really strong again if I stop and notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy since these blank moments and feelings of sadness started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. A lot, actually.\n\nI used to be the person who was always in the middle of things, not in a loud way maybe, but dependable. More like I feel foggy and frozen while everything around me keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Have these blank moments affected your relationships with coworkers or your ability to perform your job overall?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s affected it a lot. More than I wanted to admit for a long time.\n\nAt work, I used to be someone people could count on. I mean, I know the job, I’ve done the traffic-safety steps so many times, and I understand how serious it is because if you hesitate or miss something, it’s not just embarrassing, it can be dangerous. But with these blank moments, I stopped trusting myself. I’d get stuck on things like the road-closure paperwork, where I couldn’t finish it or I’d have to stare at it and keep checking because I couldn’t hold the steps in my head. And out on the road, I’d worry, “What if I freeze right when I need to signal or move people through safely?” That kind of fear made me less reliable, and I think other people noticed.\n\nWith coworkers, I started pulling back. I didn’t want them to see me mess up or have to cover for me, and I was ashamed, so I’d get quiet or avoid talking more than I had to. I could feel myself becoming someone they couldn’t depend on in the same way, and that hurt because I wasn’t trying to be careless. I just couldn’t make my brain cooperate.\n\nAnd it didn’t stay only at work. I pulled away from choir and volunteering too, and even from my family. I kept telling myself I just needed to get it together, but I wasn’t really functioning like myself. Eventually my family got worried enough that they felt like they had to bring me in for help, because I wasn’t handling things, not at work and not outside of it either.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or how you're functioning in your daily life outside of work since these blank moments started happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… outside of work it changed a lot too. I think at first I tried to tell myself it was just stress from the job, or being tired, or that I could sort of contain it there, but it didn’t stay in that box.\n\nI stopped doing things I used to show up for. Choir was one of the first things I pulled back from, because I didn’t trust myself to keep up or even just be present the way I normally would. Volunteering too — I used to care about being dependable, being someone who came when I said I would, but I started canceling or avoiding it because I felt like I couldn’t count on my own mind. It’s embarrassing to say that, because those were things that made me feel useful.\n\nAnd with my family, I withdrew. I wasn’t talking as much, wasn’t participating like I used to. I think I kept trying to hide how badly I was functioning, but that probably made it more obvious. They could tell I wasn’t myself. I’d get stuck, or overwhelmed, or just not follow through on normal things, and eventually they got worried enough that they felt like they had to bring me in for help.\n\nSo yes, it wasn’t just the road-closure paperwork or not trusting myself with the traffic-safety steps at work. It spread into the rest of my life, where I stopped being reliable and started pulling away from almost everyone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of sadness or hopelessness lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. It’s been pretty constant lately, especially in the mornings.\n\nMost mornings I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow sadness sitting in my chest before I’ve even really done anything. It’s like the day already feels ruined before I put my feet on the floor. I’ll open my eyes and for a second I’m just lying there, and instead of feeling like, okay, I need to get up and start the day, it’s more like this sinking feeling, like I already don’t have enough in me for whatever the day is going to ask from me.\n\nAnd it’s not just regular being tired or having a bad mood, at least it doesn’t feel that way. It feels deeper than that, like something is weighing me down from the inside. I don’t always have a clear reason for it right when I wake up, which almost makes it worse, because then I start thinking, “Why do I feel this awful already?” But it’s there before anything even happens.\n\nHopelessness… I guess that’s part of it too. Not every second, but when I wake up feeling like that, it’s hard not to feel like nothing is going to get better that day. It’s like I’m starting from behind before the day even begins.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about a year and two months, I think. Around that long where waking up already feeling heavy and empty became more normal than not.\n\nIt wasn’t like one single morning where I thought, “This is the start of something.” It kind of settled in and then stayed. But looking back, the heavy depression, the guilt, the low energy, and not really having much appetite… that whole pattern has been with me for about a year and two months now. Some days are a little different in intensity, but that morning sadness has been there for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Does the heavy, hollow sadness you feel in the mornings tend to stay with you throughout the day or does it change?\n\nPatient: It usually starts right when I wake up, before anything has even happened, and that’s the part that makes it feel so… unfair, I guess. Like I’ll open my eyes and there’s already this heavy, hollow sadness in my chest, and my first thought isn’t even a thought exactly — it’s more like this feeling that the day is already ruined before I’ve even put my feet on the floor.\n\nAs the day goes on, it can shift a little. Sometimes I can get distracted for a while or go through the motions, but it doesn’t feel like it completely leaves. It’s more like it’s sitting underneath everything. The morning is usually when it feels the most obvious and crushing, because there’s nothing to blame it on yet. I haven’t done anything wrong, nothing has happened, but I already feel like I’m starting the day buried under it.\n\nSo… it changes in intensity, I guess, but that heaviness tends to follow me. It’s not like I wake up sad and then by lunch I’m suddenly fine. It’s there in the background, and sometimes it comes back up really strong again if I stop and notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy since these blank moments and feelings of sadness started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. A lot, actually.\n\nI used to be the person who was always in the middle of things, not in a loud way maybe, but dependable. Like with choir, I’d be the one figuring out rides, texting people, making sure somebody’s kid wasn’t left without a way there. Or with the garden swaps, I’d be asking who had extra seedlings, who needed jars, who was bringing what. And youth basketball snacks too — I used to keep track of whose turn it was, remind the other parents, sometimes just do it myself if somebody forgot.\n\nAnd now it’s like… I see the messages come in, and I just don’t feel that push in me to answer. I can sit there for hours with my phone right next to me, or even in my hand, staring at the screen, and I know I should reply. I know people are probably waiting on me. But there’s this blankness, like the part of me that used to care enough to jump up and organize things is just not connecting.\n\nIt’s not that I suddenly hate those things or those people. That’s what makes me feel bad about it. I remember enjoying being useful and involved. I remember feeling like, okay, this is where I fit. But lately it feels far away, like something I used to do because I was a different version of myself. Now I just sit there and let the phone light up, and I don’t answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself regularly, so I can’t give you an exact number, but I’ve noticed it in my clothes. My flagger pants — the ones I wear for work — they’ve been sitting looser on me, like I keep having to pull them up or adjust them more than I used to. At first I thought maybe they’d stretched out or something, but then other clothes started feeling kind of the same.\n\nFood just… doesn’t mean much to me lately. I don’t really think about it the way I used to. I’ll realize it’s late in the day and I haven’t eaten, not because I was trying not to, just because it didn’t occur to me or nothing sounded worth the effort. Sometimes my family will ask if I ate, or they’ll say I look drawn, like my face looks thinner or tired or something, and that makes me feel embarrassed because I didn’t really notice it happening.\n\nSo yes, I think I’ve lost weight. I just don’t know how much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not exactly like I wake up somewhere and have no idea how I got there… I don’t think it’s been that dramatic. But I do have these moments where my mind just goes blank right in the middle of something I should absolutely know how to do.

That’s actually a big reason I came in. It scares me because it happens at the worst times. Like I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly it’s like the steps are gone. These are things I’ve done for years — not complicated new things, not something someone just trained me on yesterday — but in that moment I can’t remember what comes next, or I’m staring at the page and it’s like the words are there but my brain won’t grab onto them.

It feels less like I went somewhere and forgot the trip, and more like I’m standing right there, but the part of me that knows what to do just shuts off. And then I get panicky because I know I should know. I’ll think, “Come on, you know this, you’ve done this a thousand times,” but that almost makes it worse because then I’m aware I’m blanking and I can’t force it back fast enough.

So… no, I don’t think I’ve found myself in a place without remembering how I arrived. But I do lose the thread badly enough that I feel like I can’t trust my own mind sometimes, especially with work stuff where there are steps I’m supposed to follow.

I’ll stare at the paperwork or the sign, and it feels like everything I know is just out of reach. That’s really why I’m here. I don’t feel like I can keep brushing it off.\n\nDoctor: Do these blank moments seem to happen more often when you're under a lot of stress or pressure?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think pressure makes it worse, or at least that’s when I notice it the most, because there’s no room to cover for it. Like if I’m just at home and my mind goes blank, I can pause or retrace what I was doing, but at work it’s different. I’ll be looking right at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and it’s something I’ve done for years, like I should know it automatically, and suddenly I just can’t remember the steps.\n\nAnd then because I know I’m supposed to act quickly and get it right, I start panicking inside and trying to force my brain to come back online, but it’s like the harder I try, the emptier it gets. It’s not just ordinary distraction. It feels like the information is there somewhere, but I can’t reach it when I need it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed outside of these blank moments, in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that. I mean, I know I get wound up when those blank moments happen, because it feels like I’m going to mess something up or look incompetent, especially at work. But outside of that, in just normal daily life, I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly.\n\nI do feel overwhelmed sometimes, but I haven’t really separated it out in my head, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be going through the day and I know I’m tense, or I feel like I have too much to keep track of, but I don’t always notice it until something goes wrong or I freeze up. Then I look back and think, “Maybe I was already overloaded,” but in the moment it just feels like I’m trying to get through things.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. I don’t have a clear answer. I know the blanking out scares me, and the pressure around it makes me feel overwhelmed. Outside of that, I haven’t really paid enough attention to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience memory problems or difficulty recalling information in situations outside of your work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure if it happens as much outside work, or maybe I just don’t notice it the same way because there isn’t someone waiting on me or traffic depending on me. At work it’s very obvious because I’ll be looking right at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. It’s like my mind just goes completely blank at the worst possible time.\n\nOutside work… I mean, I do have moments where I feel like I lose the thread of what I’m doing, but the ones that really scare me are the work ones because they’re so basic to my job. It’s not like I’m learning something new. I know those procedures. I’ve done them over and over, and then suddenly it’s like I can’t access them. That’s the part that made me come in, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body watching yourself during these blank moments?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t think I’d describe it like being outside my body exactly. Not like I’m up on the ceiling watching myself or anything like that. It’s more that everything sort of stops in my head, and I’m still there, I know I’m there, I know I’m holding the paddle or looking at the paperwork, but I can’t connect to what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nSometimes afterward I think back on it and it feels kind of unreal, but in the moment I’m mostly just frozen and embarrassed and trying to make myself function. I’m aware of my body, like I can feel myself standing there, and I can hear things going on around me, but it’s like there’s a delay between me and my own thinking.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed the “watching myself from outside” feeling. If anything, it’s more like I’m trapped inside my head while it’s not working properly, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in a daze or disconnected from your surroundings when these blank moments happen?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes, but I don’t know if “disconnected from my surroundings” is exactly the word I’d use. It’s more like my mind just shuts off right in the middle of something I should know how to do. Like I’ll be standing there with the stop-slow paddle, or looking at the road-closure forms, and suddenly I can’t remember the next step. And these are things I’ve done for years, so it’s not like I’m confused because it’s new.\n\nI’m still aware of where I am, like I know I’m at work and I know cars are there and people are expecting me to do what I’m supposed to do. But it feels like I’m in this mental fog, or like there’s nothing available in my head for a few moments. That’s what scares me. It happens at the worst times, when I need my brain to work, and instead it just goes blank.\n\nSo maybe “dazed” fits more than disconnected. I don’t feel totally gone, but I feel stuck, like I’m there but I can’t get my thoughts to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like things around you aren't real during these blank moments?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say things around me aren’t real. I don’t think I’ve had that exact feeling, like the cars or the cones or the people around me are fake or like I’m in a dream or something. It’s more that everything is happening and I know it’s happening, but I can’t quite process it fast enough in that moment.\n\nLike, I’ll still recognize where I am. I know I’m at the job site, I know the road is there, I know there are drivers waiting, and I know I’m supposed to be doing something. It doesn’t look unreal to me, exactly. It’s just like my brain stops connecting the pieces together, and then because I’m stuck like that, the whole situation can feel strange afterward when I think about it.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’d describe it as the world not feeling real. More like I feel foggy and frozen while everything around me keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Have these blank moments affected your relationships with coworkers or your ability to perform your job overall?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s affected it a lot. More than I wanted to admit for a long time.\n\nAt work, I used to be someone people could count on. I mean, I know the job, I’ve done the traffic-safety steps so many times, and I understand how serious it is because if you hesitate or miss something, it’s not just embarrassing, it can be dangerous. But with these blank moments, I stopped trusting myself. I’d get stuck on things like the road-closure paperwork, where I couldn’t finish it or I’d have to stare at it and keep checking because I couldn’t hold the steps in my head. And out on the road, I’d worry, “What if I freeze right when I need to signal or move people through safely?” That kind of fear made me less reliable, and I think other people noticed.\n\nWith coworkers, I started pulling back. I didn’t want them to see me mess up or have to cover for me, and I was ashamed, so I’d get quiet or avoid talking more than I had to. I could feel myself becoming someone they couldn’t depend on in the same way, and that hurt because I wasn’t trying to be careless. I just couldn’t make my brain cooperate.\n\nAnd it didn’t stay only at work. I pulled away from choir and volunteering too, and even from my family. I kept telling myself I just needed to get it together, but I wasn’t really functioning like myself. Eventually my family got worried enough that they felt like they had to bring me in for help, because I wasn’t handling things, not at work and not outside of it either.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or how you're functioning in your daily life outside of work since these blank moments started happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… outside of work it changed a lot too. I think at first I tried to tell myself it was just stress from the job, or being tired, or that I could sort of contain it there, but it didn’t stay in that box.\n\nI stopped doing things I used to show up for. Choir was one of the first things I pulled back from, because I didn’t trust myself to keep up or even just be present the way I normally would. Volunteering too — I used to care about being dependable, being someone who came when I said I would, but I started canceling or avoiding it because I felt like I couldn’t count on my own mind. It’s embarrassing to say that, because those were things that made me feel useful.\n\nAnd with my family, I withdrew. I wasn’t talking as much, wasn’t participating like I used to. I think I kept trying to hide how badly I was functioning, but that probably made it more obvious. They could tell I wasn’t myself. I’d get stuck, or overwhelmed, or just not follow through on normal things, and eventually they got worried enough that they felt like they had to bring me in for help.\n\nSo yes, it wasn’t just the road-closure paperwork or not trusting myself with the traffic-safety steps at work. It spread into the rest of my life, where I stopped being reliable and started pulling away from almost everyone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of sadness or hopelessness lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. It’s been pretty constant lately, especially in the mornings.\n\nMost mornings I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow sadness sitting in my chest before I’ve even really done anything. It’s like the day already feels ruined before I put my feet on the floor. I’ll open my eyes and for a second I’m just lying there, and instead of feeling like, okay, I need to get up and start the day, it’s more like this sinking feeling, like I already don’t have enough in me for whatever the day is going to ask from me.\n\nAnd it’s not just regular being tired or having a bad mood, at least it doesn’t feel that way. It feels deeper than that, like something is weighing me down from the inside. I don’t always have a clear reason for it right when I wake up, which almost makes it worse, because then I start thinking, “Why do I feel this awful already?” But it’s there before anything even happens.\n\nHopelessness… I guess that’s part of it too. Not every second, but when I wake up feeling like that, it’s hard not to feel like nothing is going to get better that day. It’s like I’m starting from behind before the day even begins.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about a year and two months, I think. Around that long where waking up already feeling heavy and empty became more normal than not.\n\nIt wasn’t like one single morning where I thought, “This is the start of something.” It kind of settled in and then stayed. But looking back, the heavy depression, the guilt, the low energy, and not really having much appetite… that whole pattern has been with me for about a year and two months now. Some days are a little different in intensity, but that morning sadness has been there for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Does the heavy, hollow sadness you feel in the mornings tend to stay with you throughout the day or does it change?\n\nPatient: It usually starts right when I wake up, before anything has even happened, and that’s the part that makes it feel so… unfair, I guess. Like I’ll open my eyes and there’s already this heavy, hollow sadness in my chest, and my first thought isn’t even a thought exactly — it’s more like this feeling that the day is already ruined before I’ve even put my feet on the floor.\n\nAs the day goes on, it can shift a little. Sometimes I can get distracted for a while or go through the motions, but it doesn’t feel like it completely leaves. It’s more like it’s sitting underneath everything. The morning is usually when it feels the most obvious and crushing, because there’s nothing to blame it on yet. I haven’t done anything wrong, nothing has happened, but I already feel like I’m starting the day buried under it.\n\nSo… it changes in intensity, I guess, but that heaviness tends to follow me. It’s not like I wake up sad and then by lunch I’m suddenly fine. It’s there in the background, and sometimes it comes back up really strong again if I stop and notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy since these blank moments and feelings of sadness started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. A lot, actually.\n\nI used to be the person who was always in the middle of things, not in a loud way maybe, but dependable. Like with choir, I’d be the one figuring out rides, texting people, making sure somebody’s kid wasn’t left without a way there. Or with the garden swaps, I’d be asking who had extra seedlings, who needed jars, who was bringing what. And youth basketball snacks too — I used to keep track of whose turn it was, remind the other parents, sometimes just do it myself if somebody forgot.\n\nAnd now it’s like… I see the messages come in, and I just don’t feel that push in me to answer. I can sit there for hours with my phone right next to me, or even in my hand, staring at the screen, and I know I should reply. I know people are probably waiting on me. But there’s this blankness, like the part of me that used to care enough to jump up and organize things is just not connecting.\n\nIt’s not that I suddenly hate those things or those people. That’s what makes me feel bad about it. I remember enjoying being useful and involved. I remember feeling like, okay, this is where I fit. But lately it feels far away, like something I used to do because I was a different version of myself. Now I just sit there and let the phone light up, and I don’t answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself regularly, so I can’t give you an exact number, but I’ve noticed it in my clothes. My flagger pants — the ones I wear for work — they’ve been sitting looser on me, like I keep having to pull them up or adjust them more than I used to. At first I thought maybe they’d stretched out or something, but then other clothes started feeling kind of the same.\n\nFood just… doesn’t mean much to me lately. I don’t really think about it the way I used to. I’ll realize it’s late in the day and I haven’t eaten, not because I was trying not to, just because it didn’t occur to me or nothing sounded worth the effort. Sometimes my family will ask if I ate, or they’ll say I look drawn, like my face looks thinner or tired or something, and that makes me feel embarrassed because I didn’t really notice it happening.\n\nSo yes, I think I’ve lost weight. I just don’t know how much.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing something or being somewhere without remembering how you got there after one of these blank moments?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like I wake up somewhere and have no idea how I got there… I don’t think it’s been that dramatic. But I do have these moments where my mind just goes blank right in the middle of something I should absolutely know how to do.\n\nThat’s actually a big reason I came in. It scares me because it happens at the worst times. Like I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly it’s like the steps are gone. These are things I’ve done for years — not complicated new things, not something someone just trained me on yesterday — but in that moment I can’t remember what comes next, or I’m staring at the page and it’s like the words are there but my brain won’t grab onto them.\n\nIt feels less like I went somewhere and forgot the trip, and more like I’m standing right there, but the part of me that knows what to do just shuts off. And then I get panicky because I know I should know. I’ll think, “Come on, you know this, you’ve done this a thousand times,” but that almost makes it worse because then I’m aware I’m blanking and I can’t force it back fast enough.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve found myself in a place without remembering how I arrived. But I do lose the thread badly enough that I feel like I can’t trust my own mind sometimes, especially with work stuff where there are steps I’m supposed to follow.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

That’s what makes me feel bad about it. I remember enjoying being useful and involved. I remember feeling like, okay, this is where I fit. But lately it feels far away, like something I used to do because I was a different version of myself. Now I just sit there and let the phone light up, and I don’t answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself regularly, so I can’t give you an exact number, but I’ve noticed it in my clothes. My flagger pants — the ones I wear for work — they’ve been sitting looser on me, like I keep having to pull them up or adjust them more than I used to. At first I thought maybe they’d stretched out or something, but then other clothes started feeling kind of the same.\n\nFood just… doesn’t mean much to me lately. I don’t really think about it the way I used to. I’ll realize it’s late in the day and I haven’t eaten, not because I was trying not to, just because it didn’t occur to me or nothing sounded worth the effort. Sometimes my family will ask if I ate, or they’ll say I look drawn, like my face looks thinner or tired or something, and that makes me feel embarrassed because I didn’t really notice it happening.\n\nSo yes, I think I’ve lost weight. I just don’t know how much.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing something or being somewhere without remembering how you got there after one of these blank moments?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like I wake up somewhere and have no idea how I got there… I don’t think it’s been that dramatic. But I do have these moments where my mind just goes blank right in the middle of something I should absolutely know how to do.\n\nThat’s actually a big reason I came in. It scares me because it happens at the worst times. Like I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly it’s like the steps are gone. These are things I’ve done for years — not complicated new things, not something someone just trained me on yesterday — but in that moment I can’t remember what comes next, or I’m staring at the page and it’s like the words are there but my brain won’t grab onto them.\n\nIt feels less like I went somewhere and forgot the trip, and more like I’m standing right there, but the part of me that knows what to do just shuts off. And then I get panicky because I know I should know. I’ll think, “Come on, you know this, you’ve done this a thousand times,” but that almost makes it worse because then I’m aware I’m blanking and I can’t force it back fast enough.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve found myself in a place without remembering how I arrived. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really… no. I keep trying to tell myself maybe if I just show up, it’ll come back, but it doesn’t feel the same.

Like I said, I used to really get something out of being involved. Choir rides, for example — I wasn’t just doing logistics. I liked knowing people could count on me, that everybody would get there, that I was part of the group. Same with the garden swaps. I used to enjoy the little back-and-forth messages about who had tomato starts or extra jars or who needed help carrying things. Even youth basketball snacks, which sounds silly, but I liked keeping track of it. I liked being useful.

Now my phone will light up with those same kinds of messages, and I’ll just… stare at it. Sometimes for hours. I can see someone asking a normal question, something I would have answered right away before, and there’s just no push in me. No spark, I guess. I don’t feel that little tug of interest or satisfaction. It’s like I know those things mattered to me, but I can’t feel the mattering in the moment.

And then I feel guilty, because these are good people and I haven’t stopped caring about them exactly. It’s more like the part of me that used to enjoy joining in and organizing things has gone quiet. I miss that version of myself, honestly.
